- **Reading** (`internal/ups`): `Read` dials `upsd`, asks, reads one line of 512 bytes at most, says `LOGOUT`, all within 5 s; `Answer` reads that line, its value unescaped, or an `ERR` code; `Next` is the pure decision, `FSD`, or `OB` with `LB`, low; `OB` on battery; `OL` on line power; from low only `OL` goes back; a reading that says nothing keeps the power for 15 s, upsmon's `DEADTIME`, then counts a UPS on battery as low. `value.ParseNUT` reads `ups.nut`, with brackets for an IPv6 address and NUT's port 3493 by default.
- **The daemon** (`internal/daemon/ups.go`): a goroutine reads the UPS every 5 s, its first reading before the first pass. On battery, `Wake` leaves out the changed copies to read, the hashes, the backup, the scrub, balancing and the rescan, and none of their times is one to wake at; a pass asks before each copy and each transfer off the SSD cache, and balancing before each file (`jobs.OnBattery`): what does not start counts in `waiting`, with no time it is due at, and line power back makes a pass due at once. Each turn of the loop runs under a context that the battery running low cancels, as `SIGTERM` cancels a pass; the loop then parks: `syncfs` on each disk its last check found, a failed one aside, all at once for a minute at most, then nothing but the UPS, the watcher and `SIGHUP`, and back on line power a pass for `start`. The service manager is told each change (`STATUS=`).
- **The alert** `ups-unreachable` (§23), and a pass when it begins or ends, whose alerts tell of it. **The configuration**: `ups.enabled` no longer warns of a later phase; a `ups.nut` that is no address NUT can have warns when the UPS is enabled. `fsx.Dir.SyncFS`, real and with faults. `deploy/atoll.service` comes after `nut-server.service`.
- Tests: the vectors; `TestPowerVectors`, `TestAnswerVectors`, `TestNUTVectors`; `TestReadAsksUpsd`, `TestReadFails`, `TestReadGivesUp` against an `upsd` of the test's own; in `internal/jobs`, no copy on battery and the copy back on line power, a copy under way that finishes when the battery takes over during it and the next that does not start, a dry run, a transfer off the cache, balancing; in `internal/daemon`, with that `upsd` over TCP and real passes: no copy on battery and the copy on line power, no hash read on battery inside the window and the hashes read on line power, a low battery that parks the daemon, writes out both disks and holds a new file until line power, a UPS unheard on battery that parks it, a reload that turns the UPS off and ends the park, and the alert of a UPS unheard on line power, the copy made all the same; `TestSyncFS`; and `TestALowBatteryWritesOutRealDisks` in `test/loopdev`: 8 MiB written without an fsync on ext4 and XFS reach both loop devices once the battery runs low, the kernel's own writeback held off, then the daemon passes for nothing while parked, and for `start` on line power. Verified: the unit tests, the race detector on jobs, daemon, ups, fsx, alerts, watch and catalog, lint on the default and loop-device builds, 617 scenarios, and in qemu on Linux 6.12.117 and 7.3.1 `TestALowBatteryWritesOutRealDisks` with the tests of the daemon and of the SSD cache. Made to fail, each of 29 mutations was caught; the one that first lived, a UPS gone unheard that brings no pass, got a stricter test: the pool quiet first, as a pass for other events had sent the alert in its place.
- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-17** ("Oui, confirmés"):
  - on battery, the changed copies to read wait too, as they read files whole, as the hashes do; a pass for any reason still runs, the first and one after a reload, which walk every disk, included; the purge of the trash, SMART, the media servers and the alerts go on;
  - the battery running low ends the step under way, whatever it is, a mount check or a scrub's slice as a pass; a daemon whose UPS is low when it starts parks before its first pass; and back on line power it passes for `start`, as the pass the battery stopped may have been one that walked every disk;
  - the flush is `syncfs` on each disk, writes of the view and of the media servers included, and nothing of the catalog, each of whose commits is durable; the view's process runs on, as its writes are on the disks, and the machine's shutdown is upsmon's;
  - a new connection for each reading, `LOGOUT` after it; the 15 s count from the start, or from the reload that enables the UPS; no configuration key for the 5 s or the 15 s;
  - `OL` and `OB` together count as on battery, and `LB` on line power as on line power, as upsmon reads it;
  - an address NUT cannot have, a port past 65535 or with a leading zero as `api.listen` refuses it, loads with a warning rather than an error, as format version 1 takes it, and the daemon finds the UPS unreachable;
  - the alert `ups-unreachable` has the default priority, 3 for ntfy and 5 for Gotify;
  - commands run by hand, `atoll daemon --until-idle` among them, read no UPS;
  - the daemon says each change to the service manager with `STATUS=`, and `deploy/atoll.service` starts after `nut-server.service` when NUT runs on the machine.

## The SSD cache, 2026-11-17 (specs.md §4, §6.1, §13.3, §13.4, §15.12, §19, §21.1, §21.2, §24.4)

Commit "Land new files on the SSD cache, and move them to the data disks", on `beta`, the fourth piece of phase 2b (R-26). Vectors first: `cache_residency.json`, 47 cases of `plan.json`, five of `config.json`, and the cache in `view_node.json`, `view_read.json` and `view_statfs.json`.

- **The maintainer's four answers** (2026-11-17, each the recommended one): once every copy on the data disks is verified, the copy on the cache is removed, journaled, with no retention, and the copy on a data disk that takes its place becomes the user's, §6.1 and invariant 2 naming the transfer as the one removal of a user copy no command asks for; a file whose residency is over leaves in `replication.window`, all of them in one wake of the data disks a night, and above `cache.max_fill` the oldest leave at once; the residency counts from the last change of the file on the cache; and a write that runs out of space, moved to another disk as it goes, is done apart, for every disk (TODO.md).
- **Atoll's own view only.** With `view.engine = "atoll"`, a cache disk is part of the view, presented as a data disk, read from, and a branch of `atoll mergerfs-config`'s line, so that break glass shows what it holds. With mergerfs it is left out as before, and `cache.enabled = true` and the role `cache` get a warning of no effect, no longer of a later phase.
- **Landing.** A file the view creates goes to the cache disk with the most room past `min_free` when `cache.enabled` and its policy's `cache`, read before its size is known, say so; with no room there, to a data disk as before.
- **Residency and leaving**, a pure decision in `internal/cache` (`cache.Leaving`): due once `cache_min_age` has passed since the change time of the copy on the cache, or at once with `cache = false` or the cache not enabled; a due file leaves while the window is open; above `max_fill`, the oldest leave at once; `atoll replicate` makes a file it names leave at once; a `manual` file waiting for it stays.
- **The plan** (`solver.File.Cache`): a resident file keeps its copies on the cache toward no level and never as surplus, gets only what `cache.mirror` and `cache.replicate_early` ask for, never past its level, and is `pending` rather than `degraded`; a file that leaves counts its copies on the cache for nothing, reads from them first, is placed on the data disks as any file, and once those hold its level, each copy `ok` and verified, gets a `transfer` job for each copy on the cache. The plan's properties, run on 20,000 random pools with residencies, found three faults before the code was done: a mirror copy made when the copy on the cache did not count, one made past the file's level, and a pending mirror copy that held no room; each is now a vector.
- **The transfer**, a step of the pass after its copies: each file leaving the cache is planned again alone, as its copies may have just been made; the copy on the cache hands its user part to a copy on a data disk (`catalog.HandOver`, as `--move-originals` does), then goes to its disk's trash as `surplus`, a move checked against its row, and is purged from there at once (`trash.Session.Transfer`). The pass's output has `transfer` operations, `transferred` or `to-transfer` (`api/schemas/daemon.schema.json`, a value added to a closed list). A resident file counts in `waiting`, and `next_due` is the window's first opening after its residency, so that a daemon with nothing else to do wakes then. A transfer waits while an operation of the view that no pass has taken in touches its path, as the view presents the file from the disk it made it on: on real disks, a file made through the view left the cache in the pass that first found it, and the view then presented nothing at its path, for good, as its operation could no longer be taken in; `view.TakenIn` now also knows that the view presents the cache.
- **Capacity** counts every file on the cache as leaving it.
- Tests: the vectors; `TestPlanProperties` with residencies, mirror and early copies; `TestResidencyVectors`, `TestDueAt`; in `internal/jobs`, a file leaving the cache in the window, one resident, one waiting for the window, one leaving for room, the cache doing nothing with mergerfs, a copy written on the cache during its transfer, which stays, and a pass stopped by kill -9 at each of four points of the move and the purge, after which the file is on the data disks alone; a transfer waiting for the view's operation; `TestANewFileLandsOnTheCache`; `TestMergerfsLineWithTheCache`; `TestTheTreePresentsTheCacheWhenAsked`; `TestAResidencyBeginsAtTheCurrentVersion`; five scenarios, among them the transfer seen from outside, with the trees of the three disks and the journal; and `TestTheCacheOnRealDisks` in `test/loopdev`: a file written through the view lands on an ext4 cache, the passes copy it to ext4 and XFS and remove it from the cache, read through a cold cache, the view presents it as before, and plain mergerfs from the line presents the same. Verified: the unit tests, the race detector on jobs, watch, catalog, cache, solver, view and trash, lint on the default and loop-device builds, 617 scenarios, and in qemu on Linux 6.12.117 and 7.3.1 `TestTheCacheOnRealDisks` with the tests of the view, of break glass and of the read-only view. Made to fail, each of 30 mutations was caught; the two that first lived, the start of a residency with two copies on the cache and a tree that hides the cache, each got its test.
- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-17** ("Oui, confirmés"):
  - the copy on the cache goes through that disk's trash and is purged from it at once, rather than unlinked at its name: the move into the trash is atomic and checked against the copy's row, so a file written there a moment before is never the one removed; a stop between the move and the purge leaves it in the trash, which its retention or `trash.max_size` empties;
  - the residency counts from the change time of the copy on the cache, as the catalog last recorded it, so that a change of its metadata restarts it too; of two copies on the cache, from the earlier of those in step;
  - only a new file lands on the cache: a folder or a symbolic link the view makes goes to a data disk, as before; and the folders a file landed under stay on the cache once it has left, empty, as the view merges folders;
  - during its residency, never more copies than its level: with `replicas = 0` neither the mirror nor the early copy, and at `replicas = 1` the mirror before the early copy; the mirror copy is a second one, never made when the copy on the cache does not count;
  - with `cache = false` on its rule, or `cache.enabled = false`, a file on the cache is due at once, and leaves in the window: the cache empties;
  - a `manual` file waiting for `atoll replicate` never leaves the cache, not even for room; `atoll replicate` makes a file it names leave at once, at any hour;
  - a copy on the cache that is not `ok` or `surplus`, as a stale one, gets no transfer and waits;
  - a resident file is `pending`, counts in `waiting`, and `next_due` is the window's opening after its residency;
  - the plan reads a file from its copy on a cache disk before one on a data disk, both `online`, as the SSD wakes nothing;
  - `atoll capacity` counts every file on the cache as leaving it.

## Which disk spins, and the view's reads by it, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §4, §7, §10, §21.2, §24.4)

Commit "Tell which disk spins, and read from one that does", on `beta`, the third piece of phase 2b, with what phase 2a had left: `read.policy = "prefer-spinning"` and `read.classes`. Vectors first: `view_read.json`, `power_mode.json`, `block_stat.json`, `spin.json`.

- **The maintainer's three answers** (2026-11-16, each the recommended one): the kernel's counters first, then ATA CHECK POWER MODE, an SSD always spinning and a disk that gives no answer counted online; the daemon's check measures it and records it in the catalog's disk state, where the view reads it, with no new capability for `atoll mount`; with `prefer-spinning`, a copy on a disk that spins, then the first class of `read.classes`, then the original, then by position, chosen at the open and kept until the close, and with `oldest` the original alone, `read.classes` keeping a warning.
- **The daemon's check** (every 30 s): for each disk `online`, its whole disks as SMART finds them; one with no spindle, or whose completed I/O moved since the last check, or with requests under way, spins and is asked nothing; any other is asked CHECK POWER MODE through `SG_IO` (ATA PASS-THROUGH (16), `CK_COND`), its answer read from the sense data, in both formats. It writes `standby` or `online` with `catalog.SetSpin`, only from one to the other; a sync of the disks keeps `standby` where its own check finds the disk mounted.
- **The view** reads in the order of `view.ReadOrder`, and opens the next copy when one cannot be opened now; it presents the original's attributes either way. The plan already put `online` before `standby`, and the view that writes too, so a replica and a new file now go to a disk that spins, all else equal.
- **The configuration**: `read.policy` and `read.classes` no longer warn of a later phase; `read.classes` warns, as a setting of no effect, with `oldest`. The example of §24.1 and `deploy/atoll.toml` set `prefer-spinning`, so that their `read.classes` does something. Three scenarios and five vectors of `config.json` follow.
- Tests: the four vector files; `TestTreeReadsAsThePolicySays`; `TestTheSpinStateIsTheDaemons`; `TestReadClassesWarnWithOldest`; `TestTheCheckRecordsADiskInStandby` and `TestADiskThatGivesNoAnswerIsAskedNoMore`, on a machine whose whole disks a test puts to sleep; `TestCheckPowerModeOfWhatIsNoDisk`; in `test/loopdev`, `TestSpinReadsOfAWholeDisk`, on Linux 6.12 and 7.3: a loop device's counters read and move with a write, and it gives no answer to CHECK POWER MODE, so it counts as spinning. Made to fail, each of 16 mutations was caught. No disk here sleeps: the command on a real SATA disk and behind a USB bridge is to try on the maintainer's machine (TODO.md).
- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16** ("Oui, confirmés"):
  - CHECK POWER MODE is sent by Atoll itself, through `SG_IO`, rather than by running `smartctl -n standby` every 30 s: no process for each disk at each check, and no fallback that sends anything else when the bridge refuses pass-through, as smartctl may; 5 s for an answer;
  - a disk is in `standby` when one of its whole disks answers standby, as a read may need it;
  - a disk `readonly`, `draining` or `failed` is not asked, and the view counts `readonly` and `draining` as spinning, as nothing records them asleep;
  - the state is written without the catalog's lock, and a sync of the disks by a command or a pass keeps `standby`; with no daemon, the catalog keeps what the last check found;
  - a whole disk that gives no answer is asked no more until the configuration is read again;
  - the view opens the next copy in its order when one cannot be opened, rather than failing the open;
  - the example of §24.1 and `deploy/atoll.toml` say `prefer-spinning`.

## Passes that spare the disks' sleep, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §15.13)

Commit "Walk only the disks a pass is called for", on `beta`, the second piece of phase 2b (G5); tests first.

- **A pass whose every reason is `events` or `due`** walks the disks of the ripe groups someone other than Atoll made, those where the last pass's look found what the catalog does not hold yet (the outcome's `WaitDisks`: a changed copy whose row does not hold the change time its walk found, a folder renamed and left waiting, or found at neither name), and those whose watch may have missed a change, unheard since their last walk or not walked since found. Whatever the daemon asks, the pass adds the disks holding a copy whose row says it went or moved, those with an operation left planned, and those of an operation of the view not taken in. Any other reason walks every disk, as before.
- **A disk spared** (`jobs.RunLaterOn` with a `Scope`) is neither walked nor confirmed: its copies read as their rows say, as a walk of a disk where nothing changed would find them, so a deletion, a rename or a modification on the disk walked reaches it, and a stale copy there gets its overwrite. The work step opens it, which confirms it and wakes it, only when a folder job, a rename or a trash job waits for it (`TestALightPassWalksOnlyTheDisksItIsGiven`: no walk, no read past the cache). Its line says `Spared`, with its mount check's verdict; the alerts read the last walk's line for it, so `no-access` does not come and go.
- **`maintenance.rescan_every`** counts from the last pass that walked every disk, not from the last pass: light passes would otherwise put the rescan off for good.
- Tests: 7 in `internal/jobs/light_test.go`, one in `internal/daemon/light_test.go` (a change on one folder disk, heard by inotify, calls for a pass that spares the other), and `TestDaemonSparesTheDiskNothingChangedOn` in `test/loopdev`, on real disks in qemu, Linux 6.12 and 7.3, xfs watched by a filesystem mark and ntfs-3g folder by folder: a user's file on k calls for a pass that walks k alone and copies the file to v, whose writes by Atoll call for no pass; a user's file on v then walks v alone. The other loop-device tests of the daemon and the watcher pass there too. Made to fail, each of 8 mutations was caught: a scope always nil, a disk spared read as unknown, the gone copies' disks not added, a disk spared opened with no work, no `WaitDisks`, the row's change time not compared, a missing disk spared.
- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16** ("Oui, confirmés"):
  - a daemon in shadow mode (`--dry-run`) walks every disk at every pass: it records nothing, so a later pass has nothing to start from;
  - `rescan_every` counts from the last pass that walked every disk;
  - a ripe group whose every event Atoll made calls for no walk of its disk, as its journal and its catalog hold what it did; one the view made does, as its operations are taken in by a walk;
  - a disk where the last look found what the catalog does not hold yet is walked again by the next pass, whatever its reasons: a file in conflict whose changed copy waits for the user keeps its disk walked at each pass, as a pass of every disk did;
  - a disk spared that the pass opened for its work has its links kept in step then (§17), rather than at its next walk.

## The colocate and new_files strategies, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §12.4, §13.4, §21.1, §21.2, §24.4)

Commit "Place copies with colocate and new_files", on `beta`, the first piece of phase 2b (R-28), as the spec had it; vectors first.

- **The plan's best candidate** (§13.4) is now, in order: listed; in a domain with no copy, with `spread = "domain"`; with `colocate`, the disk that holds the most of the file's group; as `placement.new_files` says; `online` before `standby`; by position. 15 vectors in `plan.json`; made to fail, by taking colocate out and percent's share away, 7 of them did.
- **colocate** (`folder` and `season` alike, `show` one folder up): the group is the files of the folder whose rule says the same `colocate`, each counted once on a disk where it has a copy, in any state, or one the plan placed, so a season placed in one plan lands together.
- **new_files**: `ordered` fills the disks in the configuration's order, each to `fill_to`, a disk below it once it holds the file first, then the most free when none is; `percent` takes the most free share of capacity, compared exactly, so the disks fill alike; `most-free` as before.
- **Atoll's own view** places what is new the same way, its group read from the catalog (`TestWriterPlacesAsTheStrategiesSay`); the mergerfs line creates with `ff` for `ordered` and `pfrd` for `percent`.
- `new_files` and `colocate` no longer warn that they wait for phase 2b; the rows of §24.4 went.
- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-17** ("Oui, confirmés"):
  - `percent` means the most free share of capacity, so that the disks fill to the same share, as DrivePool's equalizer does at placement: mergerfs's `pfrd` draws at random by free space, and Atoll's plan draws nothing at random (§29.3);
  - `folder` and `season` group alike but are two groups, as two rules that say them are two settings;
  - a disk counts the files of the group with a copy there in any state, a stale one included, as the folder is there either way;
  - `read.policy = "prefer-spinning"` and `read.classes`, listed for phase 2a and not done there, come with phase 2b's disk sleep, which knows which disk spins; the view reads the original meanwhile, and both keep their warning. Done on 2026-11-16, as the maintainer answered (above).

## The contracts frozen at format version 1, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §29.3)

Commit "Freeze the contracts at format version 1", on `beta`. The maintainer chose to freeze them now, by the rules recommended ("Geler maintenant"), and confirmed every choice DONE.md still listed as to confirm, 50 sections ("Tout confirmer"): each says so now.

- specs.md §29.3 says what format version 1 holds and what a later version keeps: the migrations 0001 to 0019 never edited, the schema changed only by new ones; the on-disk layout as it is; each key of the configuration, each field of a `--json` output and of the local API, each command, flag and exit code kept, with what it takes and means; values of a closed list added, never taken away. A change that cannot keep them is format version 2, and the maintainer's.
- `conformance/frozen/v1.json` pins it: the SHA-256 of each of the 19 migrations; the shape of 149 places of the configuration and of 483 of the 31 `--json` outputs, their types, closed lists, formats and whether each is always there; the 6 routes of the local API; the exit codes; the names of the on-disk layout. `TestContractsKeepFormatVersion1` checks it. Made to fail on a copy, it caught a migration edited, a value taken from `view.engine`'s list, and a field of `atoll status --json` no longer always there.
- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-17** ("Oui, confirmés"): a configuration key may take more types and values than it did, and an output field may gain values in a closed list, as both keep what a version 1 user relies on; an output field keeps exactly its types.

## Atoll's own view writes, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §6.1, §9, §15.1, §15.5, §17, §21.2, §24.4)

Commits "Journal what the view does in view_ops", "Present the view's own operations over the catalog", "Make what the view writes on a disk", "Write through the view on a user's behalf", "Take the view's operations in at each pass" and "Serve the view that writes at view.mount", on `beta`, from the maintainer's four answers (above). `view.engine = "atoll"` is accepted: `atoll mount` serves the view at `view.mount`, in place of mergerfs, and it writes.

- **One disk at once.** A file is changed on the disk the view presents it from, or for something new on the disk with the most room past `min_free`, as mergerfs's `mfs`; a folder is renamed or removed on every disk that holds it. The passes take each change to the other copies as a change made on that disk (§15.3–§15.5), and the guards count it so.
- **The journal**, `view_ops` (migration 0019, 9 vectors in `catalog.json`): each operation journaled before it runs; a deletion or a replacement is also a `trash` operation of `ops`, so that `atoll trash` lists and restores what the view deleted; only `atoll mount`'s reprise resolves them.
- **What the view presents** over the catalog until a pass takes an operation in: a pure function, `view.Resolve`, with 43 vectors in `view_resolve.json`; what it made or wrote is looked at on its disk.
- **On the disk** (`ops`): a file, folder or symbolic link made with the folders missing on the way, as the view presents them; a rename that replaces nothing, the rename that ntfs-3g refuses done by a link; each name durable before its operation is said done; the folders' times left as a user's change leaves them, so that a media server sees the folder changed.
- **The writer** (`internal/view/writer`): names a disk holding a copy refuses, as NTFS refuses `:`, give `EINVAL`, and nothing changes; a rename onto a name presented from the same disk sends that copy to the trash, `overwritten`, then renames, as an atomic save does; onto another disk's, `EXDEV`, so that a program copies instead; a hard link `EXDEV`, extended attributes `ENOTSUP`.
- **The passes** wait at the paths of the view's operations under way; a copy the view sent to the trash is gone by the user, not an interrupted operation of Atoll's, and its deletion propagates under `deletes.direct = "restore"` too (3 vectors in `propagation.json`); an empty folder the view removed goes from the disks where it was not empty yet; and each operation is taken in once the catalog alone presents what the view presents with it.
- **The watcher** counts what `atoll mount` does as the view's, as what mergerfs does, so that it wakes a pass; it counted it as Atoll's own, which wakes none (§15.1).
- **The daemon** runs no mergerfs branches with `view.engine = "atoll"`.
- **`deploy/atoll-mount.service`** gains `CAP_DAC_OVERRIDE`, `CAP_CHOWN` and `CAP_FOWNER`, as the daemon has, to write on a user's behalf where the library's owners allow `atoll` nothing.
- Found on the way: by the linter, before any test ran, an `err` shadowed in the making of a folder on the way, which would have taken a folder just made for absent; and by `TestViewWrites` on ntfs-3g, a rename to a name NTFS refuses let through once the file had been written through the view, as the writer then looked at its original's disk alone, on ext4, and not at its other copy, still on the NTFS disk until a pass. It reads the catalog's rows at the path too now (`TestWriterRefusesANameADiskRefuses`, which failed first).
- Tests: `TestViewOps`, `TestResolveVectors`, `TestTreeOverTheViewsOperations`, `TestUserOperations` (ext4, and the rename ntfs-3g refuses), `TestWriter`, `TestWriterRefusesANameADiskRefuses`, `TestWriterReprise`, `TestAPassTakesInADeletionThroughTheView` (propagate and restore), `TestAPassTakesInWhatTheViewMade`, `TestIsMountCommand`, `TestViewEngineAtoll`; in `test/loopdev`, `TestViewWrites`, on ext4 and ntfs-3g: a file and a folder made, a rename, an atomic save, a file written in place and given a time and a mode, a folder renamed, a file deleted, an empty folder removed, a hard link and a refused name; then the passes, after which the view presents the same from the catalog alone; then break glass, plain mergerfs at the same point presenting the same. It passes on both drivers in qemu, on Linux 6.12.117 and 7.3.1, with `TestReadonlyMount`, `TestReadonlyMountAsTheUnit` and `TestViewMatchesMergerfs` again.
- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16** ("Oui, confirmés"), besides those of the decisions, below:
  - a write is journaled once for a file, from its open until a pass takes it in;
  - `atoll mount` with no point serves both the view that writes and the read-only one when both are set, in one process, and one that fails stops both;
  - the view's operations on a disk the view cannot write to now fail with `EROFS`.
- Not done, as specs.md §21.2 says: a write that meets `ENOSPC`, hard links, extended attributes, copies written in parallel in `immediate` mode, and the SSD cache (phase 2b).

## How Atoll's own view writes, decided 2026-11-16 (specs.md §6.1, §9, §17, §21.2)

The maintainer answered the four questions the write path waited on, each with the option recommended:

- **Its process**: the view that writes runs apart from the daemon, in `atoll mount`, so that the library stays while the daemon restarts; it records what it does in a table of its own, which the daemon's passes take in, so that what it made is presented at once.
- **A file written**: the original alone; the other copies are presented no more, and the pass makes them `stale` and refreshes them.
- **A deletion**: the copy presented goes to the trash at once, the others at the next pass, under the mass-deletion guard.
- **A rename that a disk holding a copy refuses**: refused with `EINVAL`, nothing changed. The TODO item that asked it is closed.

specs.md §21.2 now says how the view writes, from those answers: each operation on one disk, the others at the next pass, as for a change made directly on that disk; §6.1 item 10, §9 (`view_ops`) and §17 follow. `view.engine = "atoll"` stays an error until the write path is done.

- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16** ("Oui, confirmés"):
  - a new folder or symbolic link goes where a new file goes, the disk `placement.new_files` picks, with the folders above it made there with the mode and owner the view presents;
  - a folder renamed or removed through the view is renamed or removed on every disk that holds it at once, as through mergerfs, an exception to one disk at once: what is below a folder spans disks, and a folder renamed on one disk alone left the files below it on the others under a name the view no longer presented, while a file the view then made below the new name sat on a disk where the pass saw no rename;
  - an empty folder removed through the view stays on a disk where it still holds what the view does not present, as a stale copy on its way to the trash, and a pass removes it once nothing is left there: today no pass removes a folder Atoll did not make;
  - a rename onto a name the view presents replaces it as an atomic save through mergerfs does: the copy presented there to the trash, `overwritten`, the rename on the source's disk, and the pass takes the result for a modification. A folder is never renamed onto one;
  - the view's journal is a table of its own, `view_ops`, beside `ops`, where a deletion or a replacement is also a `trash` operation, so that the trash, `atoll trash list` and `restore` hold what the view deleted, as they hold the rest;
  - an operation is taken in once the catalog alone presents what the view presents with it, in the order of their ids;
  - left for later: a write that meets `ENOSPC`, hard links, refused with `EXDEV`, extended attributes, refused with `ENOTSUP`, copies written in parallel in `immediate` mode, and the SSD cache.

## Atoll's own view, read only, beside mergerfs, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §21.2, §24, §25, deploy/)

Commit "Serve the view read only beside mergerfs", on `beta`. The maintainer asked for it ("Oui, ajoute-la", 2026-11-16), as a process of its own ("Processus à part"): mergerfs stays the view that writes, at `view.mount`, and Atoll's own view serves the media servers, read only, at another point, so that browsing wakes no disk and each file reads from its original.

- **`view.readonly_mount`**, an absolute path, and its relations (§24.3): never on, inside or around `view.mount` or a disk's mount, and the catalog never on or inside it. 9 vectors in `config.json`; the schema and the sample configuration, which stays the spec's example byte for byte.
- **`atoll mount [<point>]`**, the name §25 had kept for the view, serves it at `<point>` or `view.readonly_mount` until `SIGTERM` or `SIGINT` (`internal/view/readonly`). It opens the catalog and only reads it, checks every disk's mount at its start and every 30 s, and reads a copy only through the root of a disk its last check found online or readonly; a disk mounted again, after a USB reset, is read from its new mount within a check. It tells systemd it is ready once mounted. Exits: 0 once stopped, 1 when it cannot start or another process unmounts the view, 2 on a usage error, a point that breaks the relations included (`config.CheckReadonlyMount`).
- **The disks' roots** (`view.DiskRoots`) are shared by the checks and the reads: a root is replaced or dropped only once no open through it is under way, so that a descriptor is never closed, and its number given to another file, under a read. Each disk has a lock of its own: with one for all, an open that hung on a disk in trouble held the next check's replacement back, and every new open, on every disk, waited behind it (`TestDiskRoots`, which failed first, with `-race`).
- **go-fuse** mounts by `mount(2)` when the process holds `CAP_SYS_ADMIN`, and with `fusermount` otherwise (it was `mount(2)` alone). A stop that finds the view still read from detaches it, as `umount -l` does, rather than wait. Both ways open `/dev/fuse`, which udev opens to every account: where it is not, `atoll mount` says so, where go-fuse said only that fusermount failed.
- What qemu taught, each a test that failed first: a view whose process was killed still answered a `stat` of its root for a second, from the kernel's cache, so whether a process serves a view is asked by opening its root; Linux 7.3 answers `ECONNREFUSED`, not `ENOTCONN`, for a view whose process died before answering its first request; and the guest of `test/qemu`, which has no udev, gives `/dev/fuse` to root alone.
- **`deploy/atoll-mount.service`**: `Type=notify`, the user `atoll`, the group `disk`, `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` and `CAP_DAC_READ_SEARCH` alone, no mount namespace (`TestNoUnitKeepsItsMountsToItself` holds it too), `Restart=on-failure`. `deploy/README.md` says how a media server reads it: its libraries at the point, `path_map`, and a drop-in that starts it after the view.
- Tests: `TestMountRefuses` (`cmd/atoll`), `TestCheckReadonlyMount`; in `test/loopdev`, `TestReadonlyMount` (views left mounted by a process that died before answering and by one killed while it served, each detached; reads from the originals; a second view at the point refused; a disk unmounted then mounted again; a stop; an unmount by another process; a tmpfs at the point refused) and `TestReadonlyMountAsTheUnit` (the view served as the unit's user with its two capabilities alone: `mount(2)`, a copy of mode 0600 read for root and refused to another account, `SIGTERM`). Both passed three times in three in qemu, on Linux 6.12.117 and 7.3.1, with `TestViewMatchesMergerfs` and `TestViewBrowsesNoDisk` once more.
- **Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16** ("Oui, confirmés"):
  - the command is `atoll mount`, the name §25 already gave the view, rather than a new `atoll view`;
  - the disks' mounts are checked every 30 s, and each root is opened again at every check rather than kept while its device stays the same;
  - only a mount of the type `fuse.atoll` that the kernel answers `ENOTCONN` for is detached at the start; a view another process still serves is refused, as anything else is, so that two `atoll mount` never take the point from each other;
  - a view unmounted by another process ends `atoll mount` with 1, and systemd starts it again 10 s later;
  - the view's root, and an entry the catalog lacks attributes for, belong to the account that serves it, `atoll` under the unit;
  - the unit's `TimeoutStopSec` is 30 s, its `RestartSec` 10 s.
- Not done: the media servers' own units are the user's to order (`deploy/README.md` gives the drop-in); the view at `view.mount`, which writes, is the rest of phase 2a.

## go-fuse for Atoll's own view, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §21.2, §29.1, §29.7)

Commit "Serve the view through go-fuse alone", on `beta`. The maintainer chose go-fuse ("soit mergerfs soit go-fuse", 2026-11-16), as `docs/research/fuse-library.md` recommended.

- The view's package, `internal/view/fusefs`, is go-fuse's side, in every build: no tag, no cgo, no libfuse. The cgofuse side, its dependency and the CI's lint of it went; they are in the history, at `b8c4e74`.
- `make qemu-build` and the CI's lint take no tag of the view's any more: `TestViewMatchesMergerfs` and `TestViewBrowsesNoDisk` are loop-device tests like the others.
- specs.md §21.2, §29.1 and §29.7, CLAUDE.md's stack, ROADMAP.md and the report say go-fuse; the question of §30 is closed.

## The daemon's mounts reach the host, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §15.13, deploy/)

Commit "Let the daemon's mounts reach the host", on `beta`. Found while designing the read-only view; the maintainer chose to drop the settings on 2026-11-16.

- `deploy/atoll.service` had `PrivateTmp=yes` and `ProtectHome=read-only`. Either gives the service a mount namespace of its own, and then what it mounts stays its own: the host never sees it, and `MountFlags=shared` cannot bring it back, as those settings make the mounts `slave` first (systemd.exec(5)). So the remount after a USB reset, as the unit ran it, mounted the disk again for Atoll alone, while mergerfs, the media servers and the host kept the dead mount. The test of the remount ran the daemon as its user with `setpriv`, never under systemd, and could not see it.
- Both settings went, and `TestNoUnitKeepsItsMountsToItself` forbids, in every unit of `deploy/`, each setting that makes such a namespace. `deploy/README.md`, §15.13 and TODO.md say why. `NoNewPrivileges` and the capabilities stay.

## What a review of the night's code found, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §4, §13.4, §15.12, §16, §21.2)

Commit "Correct what a review of the night's code found", on `beta`. An agent read the commits from `d44b573` to `25fc1be` afresh, for correctness. What it found and was so, each with a test that failed first:

- **The view presented copies that are not at their path.** A copy whose clock runs, its last look not having found it at its name, or one that has not followed its file's rename yet, and sits at its old name, still read `ok`, and could be the original: a swap of two names on one disk served the other file's content, a rename served `ENOENT` until its job ran, and a file deleted while the guard held it stayed listed. The copies that hold a file at its path are now the sources of §13.4, and the original is the oldest of them; 4 vectors in `view_node.json`, and `TestTree`. A cache disk presented too, which mergerfs's line leaves out until phase 2b; 2 vectors.
- **A corrupt file that holds a conflict was a conflict nowhere else**:
  - a copy that left it for a file row of its own took its hash, which may be another version's: the copies made from it failed their check at every try (`TestACopyOfAHeldConflictRenamedTakesNoHash`, where the file of version B stayed `pending`);
  - left with one version, by a deletion or a copy leaving, it kept the conflict, and would have been one once repaired: it now lets go of it as a conflict clears (`clearsConflict`, 3 vectors in `propagation.json`, and the same test);
  - a disk holding one of its copies was not one it depends on (`TestReadDiskHolds`), and every scrub read its copy never verified whole, to find the same conflict again (`TestAScrubHoldsAConflictFoundOnACorruptFile`).
- **The cgofuse side was built by no CI job**: the CI's lint job now installs libfuse3's headers and lints it with cgo and `-tags fuse3`.
- Left as they are, and said in the morning report: the view answers `EIO` when the disk of the copy it presents cannot be read, where another copy could be (a design question for the write path); and go-fuse's inode numbers, a 64-bit hash of the path, could in theory name two paths alike, about one chance in a billion at 200,000 paths.

## What ntfs3 with ads does with a rename onto a stream, pinned, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §10, §15.8)

Commit "Pin what ntfs3 with ads does with a rename onto a stream", on `beta`, the rest of a TODO item.

- `TestMergerfsRenameOntoAnADSStream`, in `test/loopdev`: where the kernel's ntfs3 knows `ads`, on by default, mergerfs over an ext4 branch and an ntfs3 one renames a file on both onto `m1:n`, where `m1` is another file with its only copy on NTFS. The rename succeeds, and `m1` then holds the renamed file's content, its own gone, read through a cold cache; the view lists `m1` and `m1:n`. What `docs/research/name-compatibility.md` measured by hand, and why ntfs3 with `ads` is a refused variant. Measured in qemu: it passes on Ubuntu's mainline 7.3.1, where ads is on by default, and is skipped on Debian's 6.12.117, whose ntfs3 has no ads option.
- TODO.md: the item of pinning mergerfs and ntfs3 is done.

## A TODO item found done, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §10, §15.8)

"Watch `FS_CASEFOLD_FL` on folders under `<root>/`" needs nothing more: ext4 lets a folder take that flag only when the filesystem has the `casefold` feature, and every mount check reads the superblock again, so a disk that gains the feature turns `readonly`, with the reason `casefold`. The line left TODO.md.

## Phase 2a, Atoll's own view: browsing reads no disk, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §21.2, §21.6)

Commit "Show that browsing the view reads no disk", on `beta`.

## Phase 2a, Atoll's own view: the read path, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §21.2, §29.7)

Commit "Serve the read path of Atoll's own view through FUSE", on `beta`.

- **What a path presents** (`internal/view/present.go`), pure, with 24 vectors in `conformance/vectors/view_node.json` and 5 in `view_statfs.json`: the order of the disks, mergerfs's; the first disk that holds a copy `ok` or `surplus`, or an entry, says what the path is; a file presents its original (§7), or its first copy on a `failed` disk; an entry its first disk's row; the defaults of what a row lacks; `statfs` summed over the disks that present.
- **The catalog's side** (`internal/catalog/view.go`): the rows at a path, and the names directly below a folder, found by one seek of the index a name, whatever lies below each, so that the root of a library of 200,000 files lists its few names without reading every path (`TestViewNames`).
- **The tree** (`internal/view/tree.go`): what a path presents, a folder's listing and `statfs`, from the catalog alone; only `Open` reads a disk, one name at a time below the root the mount check confirmed, following no symlink (`TestTree`). A listing reads the rows of its names 500 at a time. Measured at 200,000 files on two disks (`BenchmarkTreeAtScale`, on the maintainer's machine): what one path presents, 63 µs; a season of 10 episodes listed, 0.26 ms; the folder of 2,000 shows over 200,000 paths, 73 ms, 134 ms with two queries a name; the root, 0.09 ms.
- **Two FUSE sides, for the choice of the library**, read only, both over the same tree: every write fails with `EROFS`, the kernel checks permissions against what the view presents (`default_permissions`), every user may read (`allow_other`), and attributes and names are kept for 1 s and no absent name, as for mergerfs.
  - `internal/view/fusefs`, through cgofuse v1.6.0 and libfuse3, which the spec and CLAUDE.md name, built with cgo and `-tags fuse3`. **It does not mount on Debian 13**, nor on any system with libfuse 3.17 or later (Fedora, Arch, openmediavault 8): cgofuse loads `libfuse3.so.3` by that name alone, and libfuse 3.17 is `libfuse3.so.4`, its soname raised as releases with another ABI had shipped (its changelog). Seen in qemu: `apt-get install libfuse3-3` finds no package, and Debian 13's `libfuse3-4` holds no `libfuse3.so.3`; cgofuse's source exits with "cannot find FUSE" when that name does not load. It passes with the library given the name cgofuse asks for, in the test's guest alone (`test/qemu/run.sh` is unchanged). `docs/research/fuse-library.md` compares the two sides.
  - `internal/view/gofusefs`, through go-fuse v2.12.1 (`github.com/hanwen/go-fuse/v2`, BSD-3-Clause), which speaks the kernel's protocol itself, as the Rust version's `fuser` does: no cgo and no libfuse, mounted by `mount(2)`, which the daemon's `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` allows. Built with `-tags gofuse`. A prototype, beside the other, for the maintainer to choose.
- **`TestViewMatchesMergerfs`**, in `test/loopdev`, on every driver in qemu on both kernels, through either side: a library replicated on two disks, mounted by mergerfs from the line `atoll mergerfs-config` prints and by Atoll's view from the catalog alone, lists the same names with the same kind, mode, owner, size, time, target and content; where a replica rots, the view reads the user's original while mergerfs reads its first branch; nothing is written through the view; `statfs` answers. Through cgofuse it passes on the six drivers and both kernels, the library renamed in the guest; through go-fuse it passes 60 times in 60, five times on each driver and kernel, once a request runs to its end whatever the kernel's interrupt, as with mergerfs and libfuse: go-fuse cancels the context of an interrupted request, which the Go runtime's own `SIGURG` to a waiting thread brings about, and an open whose catalog query was canceled failed with `EIO`, in 4 of the first 12 runs. Answering `EINTR` instead, pushed first, failed a `statfs` with `EINTR` in the CI's qemu job on `b8c4e74`: the kernel does not start such a call again. So made, both view tests passed 100 times in 100, five times on each driver and kernel. The loop-device build of `make qemu-build` takes the `gofuse` tag, so that the CI runs it, with no library in the guest.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **Two new dependencies, both behind a build tag**: `github.com/winfsp/cgofuse` v1.6.0 (MIT), which CLAUDE.md names, and `github.com/hanwen/go-fuse/v2` v2.12.1 (BSD-3-Clause), for the choice above. The delivered binary holds neither: the view is in no build without a tag until the daemon mounts it (`view.engine = "atoll"`). Once the maintainer has chosen, the other side and its dependency go.
- **The mount's options**: `ro` for the read path, `default_permissions` and `allow_other`, and the caches of mergerfs's line. No `use_ino` with cgofuse: libfuse numbers the inodes, which no media server relies on; go-fuse numbers each path by a hash of it, so that a name looked up again is the node the kernel holds.

## Phase 2a, Atoll's own view: what the catalog holds of an entry, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §9, §21.2)

Commit "Record the size and owner of each entry, for Atoll's own view", on `beta`. The first step of phase 2a, which the maintainer asked for after the alpha's limits.

- Atoll's own view serves every entry from the catalog, so that browsing wakes no disk (§21.2). The catalog held a folder's, a symlink's and an ignored file's mode and modification time, never its owner, nor an ignored file's size: a listing could not say who owns a folder, nor how long an ignored file is, without looking at the disk. A walk now records the size and the owner of each entry, and so does the folder Atoll makes for a replica (`scan-entry-attributes.json`).
- §21.2 now says what the view's read path presents: a path is what the first disk that holds something there says, as mergerfs's `ff` reads it; a file presents its original, and an entry its first disk's row; what a row lacks has a default; `statfs` sums the disks from the catalog. The read path comes first, mounted read only, and `view.engine = "atoll"` stays an error until the view writes.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **`schema/migrations/0018_entry_attributes.sql`**: three columns of `entries`, `size`, `uid` and `gid`, NULL in a row a catalog made before, until a walk finds the entry again.
- **What the read path presents**, as §21.2 now writes it, where the spec said only "the original" and "every entry from the catalog": the order of the disks, mergerfs's, with `failed` ones last; a file none of whose copies can be presented is absent rather than unreadable; one link for a folder; the defaults for what a row lacks, and for `<root>`, which the catalog holds no entry of; `statfs` as mergerfs sums its branches; the kernel's caches of 1 s, as mergerfs's.
- **The read path first, read only**, with `view.engine = "atoll"` still an error: a merged view that takes no new file would stop Sonarr, Radarr and every user, so the engine opens only once the view writes.

## The watcher's three limits, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §15.1)

Commit "Wait for the other end of a folder's rename, and watch a path past PATH_MAX", on `beta`. The maintainer asked for it among the alpha's known limits.

- **A folder renamed within the root, under inotify.** The kernel queues the two ends of a rename one after the other, so a read can come between them: the folder was taken for gone, its watches removed, then watched again from scratch, listed, and every name in it reported as `created`, which costs a look at each. A read that leaves a folder moved away with no other end now waits 10 ms for it (`TestARenameInTwoReads`, which failed so). A folder that leaves the root and comes back later is watched again, as it should be: it may have changed while it was away.
- **A path past `PATH_MAX` on ntfs-3g.** No limit any more: a mark per folder goes on a descriptor opened one name at a time, and the path of an event comes from the tree of the folders marked, never from `/proc`, since the commits that opened folders from the root. `TestWatcherOnEachDriver` now makes a file 4,230 bytes deep on every driver, by each way it can be watched, and finds it at its path through a watch per folder, fanotify or inotify. Through a filesystem mark, on ext4, XFS, btrfs and ntfs3, it comes at `""`, and the disk is looked at whole, as `/proc` names no folder past `PATH_MAX`: a cost, not a loss, and Atoll never writes such a path (§15.8). Left in TODO.md.
- **The process of an event already gone** reads as `user`, and stays so. Calling it `unknown` was tried and measured wrong: a user's command, `mv`, `cp`, a shell, has mostly ended by the time its event is read, so in qemu nearly every event of a user became `unknown` (`TestWatcherOnEachDriver`, on both kernels). The view's mergerfs and the daemon outlive their events, so only an Atoll command that ended reads as `user`; its events wake a pass, which finds its operations in the journal, and tell a media server of a folder that did change. No decision differs between `user` and `unknown`. `FAN_REPORT_PIDFD` says only that a process is gone, never who it was. Recommended: leave; written in §15.1.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **The wait, 10 ms**, once a read, and only when a folder left with no other end.

## A conflict found on a corrupt file, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §4, §7, §9, §13, §15.7, §16)

Commit "Hold a conflict found on a corrupt file", on `beta`. The maintainer asked for it among the alpha's known limits.

- A file shows one state, `corrupt` first, and `files.state` was all that remembered either: a conflict found while the file was corrupt went at once. A scrub that read a copy corrupt and a copy never verified that holds another version repaired the first, and left the file `ok`, its copies different, until a scrub read the second again, a month later (`TestAScrubHoldsAConflictFoundOnACorruptFile`, which failed so); a repair by disk, `--disk`, that found a changed copy with another content while a copy on another disk was still corrupt lost it for good (`TestAConflictHeldUntilTheLastCorruptCopyGoes`). The file now holds the conflict, in `files.conflict_held`: once no copy of it is corrupt, it is a conflict. Until then it shows `corrupt`, as before, `atoll conflicts list` gives it, marked `corrupt`, and `atoll conflicts resolve` refuses it, as a repair comes first. A corrupt copy found on a file in conflict keeps the conflict too.
- Each place that finds a conflict goes through one statement, which holds it on a file corrupt; the list that the repair kept of such files, for the end of its run, goes, as the catalog holds them now. A file that holds a conflict is not queued, and keeps its original, as a conflict does.
- No conformance scenario: none can make a copy rot, as a write moves the change time and rot is a mismatch with the size and both times unchanged. The unit tests of `catalog` and `jobs` hold it.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **`schema/migrations/0017_conflict_held.sql`**, a new migration: a column of `files`, `conflict_held`, 0 or 1, 1 only while the file is `corrupt`. A catalog made before holds nothing, as nothing told it of a conflict lost.
- **`api/schemas/conflicts-list.schema.json`**: `corrupt`, `true`, present on a file corrupt that holds a conflict; such a file is listed. The output of `atoll scan` still lists the files whose state is `conflict` only.
- **`atoll conflicts resolve`** exits with 1 on a file corrupt that holds a conflict, with a message of its own, translated.

## The folders a run stopped before its end left, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §9, §17.5)

Commit "Look at the folders a run stopped before its end left", on `beta`. The maintainer asked for it among the alpha's known limits.

- A folder Atoll made goes once Atoll's own operations leave it empty, at the end of the command or pass. A run stopped before that, by kill -9 or a power cut, or that lost the disk while it removed it, left the folder there for good, as nothing recorded which folders its operations had left: the next run looked only at the folders of its own operations (`TestTheFoldersARunStoppedOnGoAtTheNext`, which failed so). The journal already names the place every move to the trash and every rename left, so each command or pass that writes now looks too at the folders that the operations done since the last look left, and `meta` keeps that last operation, `folders_tidied_op`. A folder still Atoll's and empty goes; one the user filled stays, as before.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **`schema/migrations/0016_folders_tidied.sql`**, a new migration, and a new key of `meta`, `folders_tidied_op`. A catalog made before it starts its look from its last operation: what its earlier runs left stays as it was.
- **The look moves forward only once every folder was looked at on a disk that could be written to**: a disk missing or lost during the look leaves the same operations to the next run, which looks at all of them again, those already decided included. An Atoll folder that a run could not remove then, and that the user emptied before the next run, goes.
- **A folder looked at again that is no longer Atoll's** costs one more operation `rmdir`, undone with no event, as a folder of the run itself would.
- **A scenario changed**: `daemon-removes-own-folders-crash-journaled.json` said that the folder stays after a pass killed once its removal was journaled; it now goes at the next run.

## The remount of an ntfs-3g disk, and the owner its mount gives, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §10, §15.4, §15.13)

Commit "Take the owner an ntfs-3g mount gives for the mount's, and test the remount as the daemon's account", on `beta`. The maintainer asked for the remount of an ntfs-3g disk after a USB reset to be tested, as the dedicated user.

- **The remount itself works**, as root and as the dedicated user, ext4 and ntfs-3g, on Linux 6.12 and 7.3: `TestDaemonRemountsAfterAReset` now runs on both drivers, and `TestRemountAsTheDedicatedUser` runs `umount -l` and `mount` as the unit runs them, another account with the unit's capabilities alone and no new privileges.
- **But ntfs-3g mounts again, as that account, only on a mount point it may write to**: `mount.ntfs-3g` refuses one that root alone owns. `deploy/README.md` says to give each ntfs-3g disk's mount point to `atoll`. Nothing in Atoll can do it for the administrator.
- **And the volume then shows every file as that account's.** ntfs-3g keeps no owner of its own for a file: every file shows the mount's, and a mount by `atoll` makes them all `atoll`'s. A pass found nothing to do at once, as no change time moved; but the next change of any of those files, a date set by Radarr for one, carried the owner `atoll` to the copies of the other disks, as a `chown` (`TestAnNTFSDiskMountedAgainByAnotherAccount`, which failed so before the fix). The mount check now says, from the mount table, when a disk is mounted through FUSE, as ntfs-3g and lowntfs-3g mount NTFS (`fuseblk`), and such a copy gives neither its owner nor its mode to the others: the date still goes. ntfs3 keeps an owner and a mode for each file, measured, and gives them as before.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **Every disk mounted through FUSE gives no owner and no mode**, ntfs-3g with its `permissions` option or a user mapping included, which Atoll cannot see in the mount table: a real `chown` made there does not reach the other disks. The other way would carry a mount's owner to every copy of the pool.
- **`conformance/vectors/disk_check.json` has a new field, `mount_owner`**, and `metadata.json` one too, for a copy whose disk gives its mount's owner.

## A watcher that missed a file made with its folder, on btrfs, 2026-11-16 (specs.md §15.1)

Commit "List a folder that arrives through a descriptor opened after its mark", on `beta`, which the maintainer asked for.

- The local CI of `47ab5f6` failed `TestWatcherOnEachDriver/btrfs/inotify` once more, on Linux 6.12, while another qemu run went on beside it: a file made with its folder reached the watcher as written, never as created. A folder that arrives was opened, then marked, then listed through the descriptor opened first, and btrfs lists a folder as it was when the descriptor was opened, since Linux 6.5: a file made between the open and the mark was in neither the listing nor the events. The side session had guessed so. A hook of the watcher's options, for tests, now holds that gap open, and the test makes a file in it: it failed on btrfs under inotify on both kernels, every time, and passes now, every driver and both kernels, three runs each. Each folder is listed through a descriptor opened once it is marked, under inotify and under the marks folder by folder alike, and so is every folder below it. Its write reached the watcher all the same, so a pass came: what was lost was the event saying the file was new.

## Under the 200 MB of §26 at 200,000 files a disk, 2026-11-15 (specs.md §26)

Commits "Say what a peak of memory is made of, and what brings it under the 200 MB" and "Turn huge pages off and keep 155 MiB for the Go heap", on `beta`. The maintainer asked to look for what the process held outside the Go heap, then to apply what was found.

- **What a peak is made of.** `TestScale` and the soak now read, at the most resident they see the process, `/proc/self/smaps` and what the Go runtime and SQLite's allocator hold (the test built with the tag `memory.counters`, which `make qemu-build` gives it). A pass over 400,000 copies held the Go runtime up to its soft limit of 170 MiB, SQLite's allocator 10.2 MiB at every step, the binary's pages 14.5 MiB, and up to 43 MiB more that the kernel kept resident in transparent huge pages after the runtime gave it back, up to 146 MiB of the process being in them. That last share is what moved the peaks by some 30 MiB from one run to the next, and what had been read as a rise of the link guard's passes.
- **The maintainer's decision: no huge pages, and 155 MiB.** Each command turns transparent huge pages off for its own process at start, and its soft limit goes from 170 to 155 MiB. Measured beforehand on copies of the code, two runs side by side: with huge pages off alone every step peaks under the 200 MB but the first pass with the link guard, at 191.8 MiB; with 155 MiB besides, every step peaks at 181.6 MiB at most, and no step took longer that the two runs tell apart. The tests that measure the daemon in their own process do the same as the binary. Measured on the commit itself, `47ab5f6`: every step of `TestScale` peaks at 183.8 MiB at most by the kernel's own count, for 190.7 MiB allowed, with SQLite's allocator at 10.2 MiB, the binary's pages at 14.4 MiB and 2 MiB in huge pages, and every step takes the time it took before.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **The flag reaches what the daemon starts**, `smartctl`, `mount` and `umount`, as the kernel passes it on through `fork` and `exec`: none of them holds memory for long. **A kernel that does not know the flag**, before Linux 3.15, leaves the huge pages as they are, with no warning.
- **`GOMEMLIMIT` sets the soft limit alone**: the huge pages stay off whatever it says.

## The maintainer's answers on the alpha, 2026-11-14 (specs.md §9, §10, §12.4, §13.1, §13.4, §15.5, §15.11, §17, §17.1, §21.5, §23)

His answers to the points the alpha's lines left to decide (TODO.md), each done as any fix.

- **A volume ntfs3 left with names that disagree is told of** ("Tell of an NTFS volume whose names and records disagree"). His answer: an alert, and a line in `atoll status`. A reprise that meets a name and its file's record disagreeing, as ntfs3 leaves them when a device fails during a move, leaves the operation planned and concludes nothing, as before; it now records how many it left so on the disk's row, `disks.names_disagree` (migration `0014`), and 0 once a reprise resolves them. The alert `ntfs-names:<id>` and a line of `atoll status`, `names_disagree` in its `--json`, say to repair the volume with `chkdsk /f` on Windows. Nothing changes in what is written. `TestARepriseCountsNamesThatDisagree`, through a reprise that meets them then resolves them once repaired.
- **A link of the guard that nothing names is told of** ("Tell of the links of the guard whose data nothing names"). His answer: a line in `atoll status` and an alert. Such a link, with neither a row nor a `copy-gone` event, stayed with a line of the log alone; each run of the guard now records how many it found on the disk's row, `disks.links_unnamed` (migration `0015`), for the alert `link-unnamed:<id>` and `atoll status`, `links_unnamed` in its `--json`. The data stays where it is. `TestALinkNothingNamesIsCounted`.
- **The marker is read again at each confirmation** ("Read the marker at each confirmation, and write no more to a disk of another"). His answer: read it, readonly if another. A session that may write reads `<workdir>/disk.toml` when it confirms a disk; a marker that names another pool, another disk of this pool or another filesystem, or a file there this version does not read, makes the disk `readonly`, with the reason `other-marker`, last of §10's table and of the closed lists of the `--json` outputs. Seven cases of `disk_check.json` (the field `marker`), `TestADiskOfAnotherPoolIsWrittenToNoMore`.
- **A new file a walk finds is told to the media servers** ("Tell the media servers of the folders where a walk found new files"). His answer: tell the servers. A file written straight to a disk, by hand or while the daemon was stopped, is found by a walk, which renames and deletes nothing, so no server heard of it. Each walk of a pass now names the folders where it found copies new to the catalog, and the daemon tells Plex, Jellyfin and Emby of them with the folders of its operations, held while a disk is missing as those are. `TestAWalkNamesTheFoldersOfItsNewCopies`, `TestAPassTellsTheFoldersOfNewCopies`. The loop-device test in qemu then found Plex asked for a folder written through the view, which its own watch sees: every pass walks every disk, so the walks told of whatever was new, whoever wrote it ("Tell of new copies only where no watch heard them"). Only the disks whose watch may have missed a change since their last walk are told of now: not watched all along, as from the start of the daemon, while missing or when no watch could be put, and after an overflow or a watch that ended; on a disk watched all along, the watcher's groups say what to tell, by who made the change. `TestUnheardDisks`, and `TestNotifyMediaServer` in qemu.
- **`deletes.direct = "restore"` makes the copy again in its place** ("Make a copy deleted straight on its disk again in its place"). His answer: on the same disk. It propagated nothing and only dropped the row, leaving the file below its level for the plan to place wherever. Where a deletion would have been decided, the copy gone is now made again on its disk, from the first copy found there by position, through the copy pipeline: a new action, `restore`, in `propagation.json` (the decision's `src` names the copy read), with three cases. `TestADirectDeletionIsRestoredInPlace`.
- **The topology of R-06 is looked at each start** ("Count two disks on one physical disk as one, from each start"). His answer: an alert, and they count as one. `disk add` refused a disk on a physical disk another lived on, and nothing looked again: a repartition or a change of device-mapper later went unseen, and the plan could put two copies of a file there. Each start of the daemon, the one that runs for good and each `--until-idle`, now asks every disk it finds mounted and records the whole disks in `disks.device` (there since migration 0001, never written). The plan takes one more input, `physical` on each disk in `plan.json` (five vectors): of the copies on one physical disk only one counts, the others go `surplus` and one is made elsewhere, and no disk of that group is a candidate. The capacity counts the group as one disk, and the alert `shared-device:<id>` names it; writes go on. How it sits beside the failure domains of a rule's tags: a domain stays a preference with `spread_mode = "prefer"` and a requirement only with `strict`, and is what the maintainer configures; `physical` is what the machine says, always a hard constraint, R-06 being a MUST, so it is a separate input rather than a domain. Both apply at once: a group takes the domain of its first disk in the capacity. `TestSetDevice`, `TestSharedDevices`, `TestLookAtDevices`, the property tests of the plan, and the scenario `daemon-shared-device.json`.
- **btrfs in `TestRepairOnEachDriver`** ("Repair a chunk btrfs cannot read, in the test of each driver", "Map a btrfs address through its chunk tree in the repair test"). His answer: a test. btrfs answers a read of the rotted block with `EIO`, and since a chunk that cannot be read on a disk that confirms is its copy's fault, the scrub finds the copy corrupt and the repair writes over the chunk, the copy keeping its inode and its time, its trash an empty file for the chunk. The rot goes where the chunk tree puts the address FIEMAP gives, a btrfs logical address: `btrfs-map-logical` found no extent at an address inside the file's. Passed in qemu on Linux 6.12 and 7.3, with the five other drivers.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **R-06 at each start, the details**: a group is every disk whose physical disks meet another's of it, through a third if need be, led by its first disk by position; a `retired` disk is in none, a `failed` one is not asked. **One alert per group**, not per disk. **A dry run records nothing**, so that its plan sees what the last real start found. **A disk that cannot be asked keeps what was found before**, as does every disk on a machine that cannot tell. **A copy beside a kept one is `surplus`, a user copy too**, as any copy beyond the level: nothing is removed, the file gets a copy on another physical disk, and only `atoll prune` sends an Atoll one to the trash, never the last user copy (§17.3). **In the capacity, a group spread over two failure domains** takes the domain of its first disk.
- **Restored where a deletion would have been decided, and only there**: copies `gone` with no `old_lpath`, of a file in neither `conflict` nor `corrupt`, the others peers, as §15.12 asks of a deletion; anything else drops its row as before. **No `copy-gone` is written** for it, so the mass-deletion guard does not count what is made again. **Its job is queued right after the decisions are written**, in a second transaction: a crash between leaves the file below its level, which the plan places as any. **A disk with no room for it** cancels the pending copy as §13.4 does any, and the plan places it elsewhere.
- **`atoll scan`, a command, still tells no server**, as §21.5 has every command: the decision says "a scan or a pass", and a scan run by hand records its new files, which the daemon's next pass then finds known. Telling from the command would need its notifier and its queue, which the daemon owns; to say if wanted. **A pool walked for the first time names every folder it holds**, told 30 s apart as any are.
- **The marker is read by a confirmation that may write, not by every mount check**, as "at each confirmation" says: `atoll status`, `disk list` and a scan, which write nothing to a disk, show what the check finds, and the session records `readonly` in the catalog when it opens the disk. **A file there that is no marker is another's too**, as `disk add` refuses it; one that cannot be read at all, as on a disk that fails, is logged and changes nothing, the read that follows telling of the disk. **What the check said first stays**: a disk mounted read-only keeps `read-only-mount`.
- **The links nothing names are counted as `names_disagree` is**: a column a run sets, left out of `atoll status --json` at 0; the alert is of priority 3, as nothing is at risk while the link stays.
- **A column of `disks`, set by each reprise**, rather than an event: `atoll status` reads the catalog only, and the count must end when a reprise resolves what it counted. **`names_disagree` is left out of `atoll status --json`** when it is 0, so that every other disk's line stays as it was; the alert is of priority 4, as a dirty volume's.

## The alpha's open lines (TODO.md, Phase 1a), 2026-11-14 (specs.md §9, §11.3, §13.3, §17)

- **What the alpha already did is gone from TODO.md** ("Drop from TODO.md what the alpha already does"): `disk add` and `disk remove` editing the configuration (1df7bb1), the fallbacks of §12.2 for the modes, which the jobs act on (3943dc0, 8321808), `disk show` giving `time_precision_ns` (d9f875d), and the grouping of events per path (06a4b1b).
- **A catalog rebuild lists the disks' trash again** ("List the disks' trash again after a catalog rebuild"). A catalog made from the disks knew no entry of their trash: none was ever restored or purged, so a trash filled up until someone emptied it by hand, and the journal could not answer where a copy found gone went. Each operation's folder that holds one file is now an entry of the reason `unknown`, and each repair's folder of chunks one of the reason `repaired`, with an operation `done` of the id their folder has, before the full scan. Scenario `catalog-rebuild-trash.json`, two vectors in `trash_purge.json` and one in `catalog.json`; `catalog-rebuild.json` and `catalog-ops-past-a-repair.json` give the count.
- **A plain `atoll balance` says what `--move-originals` would move** ("Say what --move-originals would move in a plain balance"): it counted only the Atoll copies, and a user copy that breaks a strict setting, which only `--move-originals` moves, went unsaid, though §13.4 says the solver suggests it. It now counts them, `originals` in its `--json`, with a line of its text; the balance scenarios give the count of each run.
- **`atoll rules check` tells of the rules an earlier one hides and the rules no file reaches** ("Tell of the rules no file reaches and the rules an earlier one hides"), as §11.3 said it would once it could read the catalog: `hidden` and `unreached` in its `--json`, a line each in its text. The pure part is in `internal/rules` (`Hidden`, `Reach`), with `conformance/vectors/rules_check.json`; scenario `rules-check-catalog.json`, and every scenario of `rules check` gives the two fields.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **What "simple cases" are**: an earlier rule's conditions each written the same in the later one, or holding for every file, and its size range around the later's; `Films/**` before `Films/Action/**` is left to `unreached`, which the catalog answers. Two new fields of `api/schemas/rules-check.schema.json`, required: `hidden`, and `unreached`, `null` when the catalog cannot be read. A file a rule ignores has no size in the catalog, so it counts as reaching each rule that may match it before the first with no size condition: a rule is never told unreached when a file may reach it. **The catalog is read without its lock**, as `names check` reads it: a command writing meanwhile changes a file more or less.
- **A count, not a list**: `originals` counts the user copies; their paths are what `atoll balance --move-originals --dry-run` lists, as moves. A field of `api/schemas/balance.schema.json`, required, as each other is; **no exit code changes** for it, as nothing a plain run can do moves them; and it is counted on every plain run, `--yes` included, at the price of one more plan of the pool.
- **A reason of its own, `unknown`**, a new value of `trash.reason` (migration `0013_trash_unknown`, which makes the table again, as SQLite changes a `CHECK` no other way) and of `api/schemas/trash-list.schema.json`, and **a field `trash_listed`** in `api/schemas/catalog-rebuild.schema.json`. `deleted` would have needed no contract, and would have said what nobody knows. An entry of the reason `unknown` never goes for room before its time, as a deleted or renamed one would not: it may be either.
- **Its time is the end of its folder's date, or the rebuild's time when that is earlier**, and it expires the retention after it: never purged sooner than it would have been, by `--older-than` either; the date is all a disk says of it.
- **What is not exactly an entry is left out**: an operation's folder with no file, two files, or anything but files and folders below it; a repair's whose chunks are not those of one file. They stay as they are, with a warning, and the journal's fallback still walks a trash that holds them (§15.5). So is a missing disk's trash, with a warning that says to rebuild again.
- **No event** is written for an entry listed again: a rebuild loses the history anyway, and none was destroyed.

## Two more decisions of the maintainer, made on 2026-11-14 (specs.md §10, §15.4, §15.6)

The measure of decision 10 showed more to decide; the maintainer answered on 2026-11-14 (TODO.md, 17 to 20).

- **An ext4 made without a journal is refused at `disk add`, and `readonly` when found later** ("Refuse an ext4 without a journal"), as `casefold` is (§10). The bit `0x4` of the compatible features at 1116, `has_journal`, is read with the superblock, a field `no_journal` of `superblock.json`, and the reason `no-journal` comes after `ascii-ci` in §10's table and in the closed lists of the `--json` outputs. Measured: an ext4 without its journal, a device failing right after a folder of 300 new names was synced, kept the names and the count of names of 1 file of 300, and needed `e2fsck`; removing one of those names frees a file another still leads to. `disk add` says why, and that `tune2fs -O has_journal` gives it one.
- **A changed copy whose change time did not move past its row's makes its file a `conflict`** ("Call a copy no user changed a conflict"). As decided: no user's change, as any change moves the change time forward and no program sets it back; a power cut that left a file empty or short, or a disk put back from an older image. Its file is a `conflict`, every version kept, for `conflicts resolve`, never `modified`, which made the other copies stale and copied over them what no user wrote. A row of §15.4's table, after the one of a stale copy, and a field `ctime_not_later` of each changed copy in `modification.json`, nine cases more. Through a real pass (`TestACopyNoUserChangedIsAConflict`), and on each driver a disk put back from an image taken before a change (`TestADiskPutBackFromAnOlderImage`, loop-device suite).

- **`MakeLink` keeps the sync of each file it links**, as it is: the prepared change (`decision-10-prepared.patch`) is not applied. Half an hour once, when the guard is turned on; nothing for a pool of NTFS disks.
- **CLAUDE.md's invariant 4** says that a read that fails on a disk that confirms right after is the chunk's fault: the copy is `corrupt` (§16), as decision 1 has it.

### Decided without the maintainer, confirmed on 2026-11-16 ("Tout confirmer")

- **The change time is compared exactly, and "not later" includes equal**: a copy's row and its look come from the same disk. Linux 6.12 can leave a change time the same within a clock tick, so a change made within the tick of the look that recorded the row reads as no user's and makes a `conflict`: a question to the user, never a loss.
- **It holds with no other copy too**, a file `x1` left empty by a power cut: nothing says that what it holds is the file's, and the alert of `files-conflict` tells of it, where a `modified` said nothing. And **it holds whatever a read would say**: nothing is read, the content of the reference included, so a copy of an older image whose content is the file's, as one whose mode alone changed since, is a conflict too.
- **A row whose change time is unknown compares as later**: no conflict from a row that never had one.
- **§15.6's example of an older image under a confirm is replaced**: such a disk no longer reaches the guard.
- **`no_journal` is read only of an ext4**, and a head of zeros at 1116 holds no journal: the ext4 cases of `superblock.json` written before, whose heads are zeros there, now say `no_journal`, and two of their notes say why. Two new cases are measured, from `mkfs.ext4` 1.47.2 with and without its journal (`0x103c` and `0x38`).
- **`no-journal` comes after `casefold` and `ascii-ci`, and before `ignore-case` and `read-only-mount`**: what the superblock says, together. An ext4 with `casefold` and no journal is told of as `casefold`; either refuses it at `disk add`.
- **Its refusal has a message of its own**, not the one of the name rules: "Atoll cannot make what it writes there durable".

## The review's sixteen decisions, made by the maintainer on 2026-11-13 (specs.md §9, §10, §12.4, §13.4, §15.4, §15.5, §15.6, §15.7, §15.10, §15.13, §16, §16.1, §17, §17.1, §23, §25, §26)

Each point the review left to decide was put to the maintainer as a question, with its options and what each costs; TODO.md lists his sixteen answers. What follows is done, each after a test that fails without it.

- **A chunk that cannot be read is its copy's fault, on a disk that confirms** ("Call a chunk that cannot be read corrupt, on a disk that confirms"). His answer: confirm the disk, then repair. A sector the disk no longer reads answers `EIO` to the scrub. The scrub took it for the disk's: the disk went `missing`, came back at the next mount check, and the next slice read the same copy first, without end, its branch out of mergerfs and back each time, the copy never named nor mended.
